<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glossary_of_artificial_intelligence" title="Glossary of artificial intelligence">Glossary</a></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><span class="mw-empty-elt"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" about="#mwt11" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Hlist/styles.css&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1239400231" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" about="#mwt12" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Module:Navbar/styles.css&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output print{.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:none!important}}</style></span><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template:Artificial_intelligence" title="Template:Artificial intelligence"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk:Artificial_intelligence" title="Template talk:Artificial intelligence"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Artificial_intelligence" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Artificial intelligence"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p id="mwBg"><b id="mwBw">Natural language processing</b> (<b id="mwCA">NLP</b>) is the processing of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_language" title="Natural language" id="mwCQ">natural language</a> information by a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer" title="Computer" id="mwCg">computer</a>. NLP is a subfield of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer_science" title="Computer science" id="mwCw">computer science</a> and is closely associated with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence" title="Artificial intelligence" id="mwDA">artificial intelligence</a>. NLP is also related to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_retrieval" title="Information retrieval" id="mwDQ">information retrieval</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_representation_and_reasoning" title="Knowledge representation and reasoning" id="mwDg">knowledge representation</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_linguistics" title="Computational linguistics" id="mwDw">computational linguistics</a>, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistics" title="Linguistics" id="mwEA">linguistics</a> more broadly.<sup about="#mwt15" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-nlpintro_1-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;nlpintro&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-nlpintro-1&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-nlpintro-1" id="mwEQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwEg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwEw">[</span>1<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwFA">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mwFQ">Major processing tasks in an NLP system include: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speech_recognition" title="Speech recognition" id="mwFg">speech recognition</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Text_classification" title="Text classification" class="mw-redirect" id="mwFw">text classification</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_language_understanding" title="Natural language understanding" id="mwGA">natural language understanding</a>, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_language_generation" title="Natural language generation" id="mwGQ">natural language generation</a>.</p>

<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" id="mwGg" data-mw="{&quot;autoGenerated&quot;:true}"></section><section data-mw-section-id="1" id="mwGw" aria-labelledby="History"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="History">History</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: History"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt16" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwHA" data-mw="{&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Further&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Further&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;History of natural language processing&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1368532237" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" about="#mwt17" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Module:Hatnote/styles.css&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+.mw-empty-elt+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}@media print{body.ns-0 .mw-parser-output .hatnote{display:none!important}}</style></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt16" id="mwHQ">Further information: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_natural_language_processing" title="History of natural language processing">History of natural language processing</a></div>

<p id="mwHg">Natural language processing has its roots in the 1950s.<sup about="#mwt20" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-2" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-2&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-2" id="mwHw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwIA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwIQ">[</span>2<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwIg">]</span></span></a></sup> Already in 1950, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alan_Turing" title="Alan Turing" id="mwIw">Alan Turing</a> published an article titled "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computing_Machinery_and_Intelligence" title="Computing Machinery and Intelligence" id="mwJA">Computing Machinery and Intelligence</a>," which proposed what is now called the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turing_test" title="Turing test" id="mwJQ">Turing test</a> as a criterion of intelligence, though at the time that was not articulated as a problem separate from artificial intelligence. The proposed test includes a task that involves the automated interpretation and generation of natural language.</p>

<section data-mw-section-id="2" id="mwJg" aria-labelledby="Symbolic_NLP_(1950s_–_early_1990s)"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Symbolic_NLP_(1950s_–_early_1990s)"><span id="Symbolic_NLP_.281950s_.E2.80.93_early_1990s.29" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Symbolic NLP (1950s – early 1990s)</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Symbolic NLP (1950s – early 1990s)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size skin-invert-image" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwJw"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AST_Document.svg" class="mw-file-description" id="mwKA"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:AST_Document.svg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/AST_Document.svg/250px-AST_Document.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/09/AST_Document.svg/500px-AST_Document.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="1052" data-file-height="744" data-file-type="drawing" height="177" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwKQ"></a><figcaption id="mwKg">A document parsed into an abstract syntax tree</figcaption></figure>
<p id="mwKw">The premise of symbolic NLP is often illustrated using <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Searle_(American_philosopher)" title="John Searle (American philosopher)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwLA">John Searle's Chinese room</a> thought experiment: Given a collection of rules (e.g., a Chinese phrasebook, with questions and matching answers), the computer emulates natural language understanding (or other NLP tasks) by applying those rules to the data it confronts.</p>

<ul id="mwLQ"><li id="mwLg"><b id="mwLw">1950s</b>: The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown-IBM_experiment" title="Georgetown-IBM experiment" class="mw-redirect" id="mwMA">Georgetown experiment</a> in 1954 involved fully <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automatic_translation" title="Automatic translation" class="mw-redirect" id="mwMQ">automatic translation</a> of more than sixty Russian sentences into English. The authors claimed that within three or five years, machine translation would be a solved problem.<sup about="#mwt24" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-3" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-3&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-3" id="mwMg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwMw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwNA">[</span>3<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwNQ">]</span></span></a></sup>  However, real progress was much slower, and after the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ALPAC" title="ALPAC" id="mwNg">ALPAC report</a> in 1966, which found that ten years of research had failed to fulfill the expectations, funding for machine translation was dramatically reduced. Little further research in machine translation was conducted in America (though some research continued elsewhere, such as Japan and Europe<sup about="#mwt25" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-4" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-4&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-4" id="mwNw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwOA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwOQ">[</span>4<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwOg">]</span></span></a></sup>) until the late 1980s when the first <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistical_machine_translation" title="Statistical machine translation" id="mwOw">statistical machine translation</a> systems were developed.</li>
<li id="mwPA"><b id="mwPQ">1960s</b>: Some notably successful natural language processing systems developed in the 1960s were <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SHRDLU" title="SHRDLU" id="mwPg">SHRDLU</a>, a natural language system working in restricted "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blocks_world" title="Blocks world" id="mwPw">blocks worlds</a>" with restricted vocabularies, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ELIZA" title="ELIZA" id="mwQA">ELIZA</a>, a simulation of Rogerian psychotherapy, written by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Weizenbaum" title="Joseph Weizenbaum" id="mwQQ">Joseph Weizenbaum</a> between 1964 and 1966. Despite using minimal information about human thought or emotion, ELIZA was able to produce interactions that appeared human-like. When the "patient" exceeded the very small knowledge base, ELIZA might provide a generic response, for example, responding to "My head hurts" with "Why do you say your head hurts?". Ross Quillian's successful work on natural language was demonstrated with a vocabulary of only <i id="mwQg">twenty</i> words, because that was all that would fit in a computer memory at the time.<sup about="#mwt28" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-5" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-5&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-5" id="mwQw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwRA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwRQ">[</span>5<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwRg">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwRw"><b id="mwSA">1970s</b>: During the 1970s, many programmers began to write "conceptual <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontology_(information_science)" title="Ontology (information science)" id="mwSQ">ontologies</a>", which structured real-world information into computer-understandable data.  Examples are MARGIE (Schank, 1975), SAM (Cullingford, 1978), PAM (Wilensky, 1978), TaleSpin (Meehan, 1976), QUALM (Lehnert, 1977), Politics (Carbonell, 1979), and Plot Units (Lehnert 1981). During this time, the first <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatterbots" title="Chatterbots" class="mw-redirect" id="mwSg">chatterbots</a> were written (e.g., <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PARRY" title="PARRY" id="mwSw">PARRY</a>).</li>
<li id="mwTA"><b id="mwTQ">1980s</b>: The 1980s and early 1990s mark the heyday of symbolic methods in NLP. Focus areas of the time included research on rule-based parsing (e.g., the development of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head-driven_phrase_structure_grammar" title="Head-driven phrase structure grammar" id="mwTg">HPSG</a> as a computational operationalization of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generative_grammar" title="Generative grammar" id="mwTw">generative grammar</a>), morphology (e.g., two-level morphology<sup about="#mwt31" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-6" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-6&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-6" id="mwUA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwUQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwUg">[</span>6<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwUw">]</span></span></a></sup>), semantics (e.g., <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesk_algorithm" title="Lesk algorithm" id="mwVA">Lesk algorithm</a>), reference (e.g., within Centering Theory<sup about="#mwt32" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-7" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-7&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-7" id="mwVQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwVg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwVw">[</span>7<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwWA">]</span></span></a></sup>) and other areas of natural language understanding (e.g., in the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetorical_structure_theory" title="Rhetorical structure theory" id="mwWQ">Rhetorical Structure Theory</a>). Other lines of research were continued, e.g., the development of chatterbots with <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racter" title="Racter" id="mwWg">Racter</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabberwacky" title="Jabberwacky" id="mwWw">Jabberwacky</a>. An important development (that eventually led to the statistical turn in the 1990s) was the rising importance of quantitative evaluation in this period.<sup about="#mwt35" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-8" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-8&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-8" id="mwXA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwXQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwXg">[</span>8<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwXw">]</span></span></a></sup></li></ul>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="3" id="mwYA" aria-labelledby="Statistical_NLP_(1990s–present)"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Statistical_NLP_(1990s–present)"><span id="Statistical_NLP_.281990s.E2.80.93present.29" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Statistical NLP (1990s–present)</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Statistical NLP (1990s–present)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwYQ">Up until the 1980s, most natural language processing systems were based on complex sets of hand-written rules. Starting in the late 1980s, however, there was a revolution in natural language processing with the introduction of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_learning" title="Machine learning" id="mwYg">machine learning</a> algorithms for language processing. This shift was influenced by increasing computational power (see <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moore's_law" title="Moore's law" id="mwYw">Moore's law</a>) and a decline in the dominance of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chomskyan" title="Chomskyan" class="mw-redirect" id="mwZA">Chomskyan</a> linguistic theories (e.g. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transformational_grammar" title="Transformational grammar" id="mwZQ">transformational grammar</a>), whose theoretical underpinnings discouraged the sort of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corpus_linguistics" title="Corpus linguistics" id="mwZg">corpus linguistics</a> that underlies the machine-learning approach to language processing.<sup about="#mwt36" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-9" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-9&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-9" id="mwZw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwaA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwaQ">[</span>9<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwag">]</span></span></a></sup></p> 
<ul id="mwaw"><li id="mwbA"><b id="mwbQ">1990s:</b> Many early successes of statistical NLP occurred in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_translation" title="Machine translation" id="mwbg">machine translation</a>, particularly through work at <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBM_Research" title="IBM Research" id="mwbw">IBM Research</a>. An IBM team including <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Jelinek" title="Frederick Jelinek" id="mwcA">Frederick Jelinek</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_F._Brown?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Peter F. Brown (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" id="mwcQ" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;Peter F. Brown&quot;]}}">Peter F. Brown</a>, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Mercer" title="Robert Mercer" id="mwcg">Robert Mercer</a> developed a probabilistic approach to translation, described in the 1990 paper <i id="mwcw">A Statistical Approach to Machine Translation</i>.<sup about="#mwt39" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Brown1990_10-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;Brown1990&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-Brown1990-10&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-Brown1990-10" id="mwdA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwdQ"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwdg">[</span>10<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwdw">]</span></span></a></sup> The work used parallel English and French text from the proceedings of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada" id="mweA">Parliament of Canada</a> and was subsequently developed into the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IBM_alignment_models" title="IBM alignment models" id="mweQ">IBM alignment models</a>.<sup about="#mwt42" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Jelinek2009_11-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;Jelinek2009&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-Jelinek2009-11&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-Jelinek2009-11" id="mweg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwew"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwfA">[</span>11<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwfQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt45" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-Brown1993_12-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;Brown1993&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-Brown1993-12&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-Brown1993-12" id="mwfg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwfw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwgA">[</span>12<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwgQ">]</span></span></a></sup> However, such systems often depended on large task-specific corpora, making the availability of suitable training data an important limitation.</li>
<li id="mwgg"><b id="mwgw">2000s</b>: With the growth of the web, increasing amounts of raw (unannotated) language data have become available since the mid-1990s. Research has thus increasingly focused on <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unsupervised_learning" title="Unsupervised learning" id="mwhA">unsupervised</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-supervised_learning" title="Semi-supervised learning" class="mw-redirect" id="mwhQ">semi-supervised learning</a> algorithms. Such algorithms can learn from data that has not been hand-annotated with the desired answers or using a combination of annotated and non-annotated data. Generally, this task is much more difficult than <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supervised_learning" title="Supervised learning" id="mwhg">supervised learning</a>, and typically produces less accurate results for a given amount of input data. However, large quantities of non-annotated data are available (including, among other things, the entire content of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Web" title="World Wide Web" id="mwhw">World Wide Web</a>), which can often make up for the worse efficiency if the algorithm used has a low enough <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_complexity" title="Time complexity" id="mwiA">time complexity</a> to be practical.</li>
<li id="mwiQ"><b id="mwig">2003:</b> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_n-gram_language_model" title="Word n-gram language model" id="mwiw">word n-gram model</a>, at the time the best statistical algorithm, is outperformed by a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-layer_perceptron" title="Multi-layer perceptron" class="mw-redirect" id="mwjA">multi-layer perceptron</a> (with a single hidden layer and context length of several words, trained on up to 14 million words, by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yoshua_Bengio" title="Yoshua Bengio" id="mwjQ">Bengio</a> et al.)<sup about="#mwt48" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-13" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-13&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-13" id="mwjg"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwjw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwkA">[</span>13<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwkQ">]</span></span></a></sup></li>
<li id="mwkg"><b id="mwkw">2010:</b> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tomáš_Mikolov" title="Tomáš Mikolov" id="mwlA">Tomáš Mikolov</a> (then a PhD student at <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brno_University_of_Technology" title="Brno University of Technology" id="mwlQ">Brno University of Technology</a>) with co-authors applied a simple <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recurrent_neural_network" title="Recurrent neural network" id="mwlg">recurrent neural network</a> with a single hidden layer to language modeling,<sup about="#mwt52" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-14" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-14&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-14" id="mwlw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwmA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwmQ">[</span>14<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwmg">]</span></span></a></sup> and in the following years he went on to develop <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word2vec" title="Word2vec" id="mwmw">Word2vec</a>. In the 2010s, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representation_learning" title="Representation learning" id="mwnA">representation learning</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_learning" title="Deep learning" id="mwnQ">deep neural network</a>-style (featuring many hidden layers) machine learning methods became widespread in natural language processing. This shift gained momentum due to results showing that such techniques<sup about="#mwt55" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-goldberg:nnlp17_15-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;goldberg:nnlp17&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-goldberg:nnlp17-15&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-goldberg:nnlp17-15" id="mwng"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwnw"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwoA">[</span>15<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwoQ">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt58" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-goodfellow:book16_16-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;goodfellow:book16&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-goodfellow:book16-16&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-goodfellow:book16-16" id="mwog"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwow"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwpA">[</span>16<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwpQ">]</span></span></a></sup> can achieve state-of-the-art results in many natural language tasks, e.g., in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_modeling" title="Language modeling" class="mw-redirect" id="mwpg">language modeling</a><sup about="#mwt61" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-jozefowicz:lm16_17-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;jozefowicz:lm16&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-jozefowicz:lm16-17&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-jozefowicz:lm16-17" id="mwpw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwqA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwqQ">[</span>17<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwqg">]</span></span></a></sup> and parsing.<sup about="#mwt64" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-choe:emnlp16_18-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;choe:emnlp16&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-choe:emnlp16-18&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-choe:emnlp16-18" id="mwqw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwrA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwrQ">[</span>18<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwrg">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt67" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-vinyals:nips15_19-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;vinyals:nips15&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-vinyals:nips15-19&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-vinyals:nips15-19" id="mwrw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwsA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwsQ">[</span>19<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwsg">]</span></span></a></sup> This is increasingly important <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence_in_healthcare" title="Artificial intelligence in healthcare" id="mwsw">in medicine and healthcare</a>, where NLP helps analyze notes and text in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_health_record" title="Electronic health record" id="mwtA">electronic health records</a> that would otherwise be inaccessible for study when seeking to improve care<sup about="#mwt70" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-20" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-20&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-20" id="mwtQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwtg"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwtw">[</span>20<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwuA">]</span></span></a></sup> or protect patient privacy.<sup about="#mwt73" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-21" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-21&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-21" id="mwuQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwug"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwuw">[</span>21<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwvA">]</span></span></a></sup></li></ul>

</section></section><section data-mw-section-id="4" id="mwvQ" aria-labelledby="Approaches:_Symbolic,_statistical,_neural_networks"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Approaches:_Symbolic,_statistical,_neural_networks"><span id="Approaches:_Symbolic.2C_statistical.2C_neural_networks" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Approaches: Symbolic, statistical, neural networks<span class="anchor" id="Statistical_natural_language_processing_(SNLP)" about="#mwt74" typeof="mw:Transclusion" data-mw="{&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;anchor&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Anchor&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Statistical natural language processing (SNLP)&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"></span></h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Approaches: Symbolic, statistical, neural networks"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mwvg">Symbolic approach, i.e., the hand-coding of a set of rules for manipulating symbols, coupled with a dictionary lookup, was historically the first approach used both by AI in general and by NLP in particular:<sup about="#mwt77" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-winograd:shrdlu71_22-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;winograd:shrdlu71&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-winograd:shrdlu71-22&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-winograd:shrdlu71-22" id="mwvw"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwwA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwwQ">[</span>22<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwwg">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt80" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-schank77_23-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;schank77&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-schank77-23&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-schank77-23" id="mwww"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwxA"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwxQ">[</span>23<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwxg">]</span></span></a></sup> such as by writing grammars or devising heuristic rules for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stemming" title="Stemming" id="mwxw">stemming</a>.</p>

<p id="mwyA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_learning" title="Machine learning" id="mwyQ">Machine learning</a> approaches, which include both statistical and neural networks, on the other hand, have many advantages over the symbolic approach:</p>

<ul id="mwyg"><li id="mwyw">both statistical and neural network methods tend to focus more on the most common cases extracted from a corpus of texts, whereas the rule-based approach needs to provide rules for both rare and common cases equally.</li>
<li id="mwzA"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language_model" title="Language model" id="mwzQ">language models</a>, produced by either statistical or neural network methods, are more robust to both unfamiliar (e.g. containing words or structures that have not been seen before) and erroneous input (e.g. with misspelled words or words accidentally omitted) in comparison to the rule-based systems, which are also more costly to produce.</li>
<li id="mwzg">the larger such a (probabilistic) language model is, the more accurate it becomes, in contrast to rule-based systems that can gain accuracy only by increasing the amount and complexity of the rules leading to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intractable_problem" title="Intractable problem" class="mw-redirect" id="mwzw">intractability</a> problems.</li></ul>

<p id="mw0A">Rule-based systems are commonly used:</p>

<ul id="mw0Q"><li id="mw0g">when the amount of training data is insufficient to successfully apply machine learning methods, e.g., for the machine translation of low-resource languages such as provided by the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apertium" title="Apertium" id="mw0w">Apertium</a> system,</li>
<li id="mw1A">for preprocessing in NLP pipelines, e.g., <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokenization_(lexical_analysis)" title="Tokenization (lexical analysis)" class="mw-redirect" id="mw1Q">tokenization</a>, or</li>
<li id="mw1g">for post-processing and transforming the output of NLP pipelines, e.g., for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knowledge_extraction" title="Knowledge extraction" id="mw1w">knowledge extraction</a> from syntactic parses.</li></ul>

<section data-mw-section-id="5" id="mw2A" aria-labelledby="Statistical_approach"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Statistical_approach">Statistical approach</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Statistical approach"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mw2Q">In the late 1980s and mid-1990s, the statistical approach ended a period of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AI_winter" title="AI winter" id="mw2g">AI winter</a>, which was caused by the inefficiencies of the rule-based approaches.<sup about="#mwt81" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-johnson:eacl:ilcl09_24-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;johnson:eacl:ilcl09&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-johnson:eacl:ilcl09-24&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-johnson:eacl:ilcl09-24" id="mw2w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw3A"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw3Q">[</span>24<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw3g">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt82" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-resnik:langlog11_25-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;resnik:langlog11&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-resnik:langlog11-25&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-resnik:langlog11-25" id="mw3w"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw4A"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw4Q">[</span>25<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw4g">]</span></span></a></sup></p>

<p id="mw4w">The earliest <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decision_tree" title="Decision tree" id="mw5A">decision trees</a>, producing systems of hard <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conditional_(computer_programming)#If–then(–else)" title="Conditional (computer programming)" id="mw5Q">if–then rules</a>, were still very similar to the old rule-based approaches.
Only the introduction of hidden <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markov_model" title="Markov model" id="mw5g">Markov models</a>, applied to part-of-speech tagging, announced the end of the old rule-based approach.</p>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="6" id="mw5w" aria-labelledby="Neural_networks"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Neural_networks">Neural networks</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Neural networks"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt83" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mw6A" data-mw="{&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Further&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Further&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{&quot;1&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Artificial neural network&quot;}},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1368532237" about="#mwt84" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Module:Hatnote/styles.css&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}"></span><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable" about="#mwt83" id="mw6Q">Further information: <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_neural_network" title="Artificial neural network" class="mw-redirect">Artificial neural network</a></div>
<p id="mw6g">A major drawback of statistical methods is that they require elaborate <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feature_engineering" title="Feature engineering" id="mw6w">feature engineering</a>. Since 2015,<sup about="#mwt87" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-26" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-26&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-26" id="mw7A"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw7Q"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw7g">[</span>26<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw7w">]</span></span></a></sup> <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_network_(machine_learning)" title="Neural network (machine learning)" id="mw8A">neural network</a>–based methods have increasingly replaced traditional statistical approaches, using <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semantic_networks" title="Semantic networks" class="mw-redirect" id="mw8Q">semantic networks</a><sup about="#mwt90" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-27" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-27&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-27" id="mw8g"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mw8w"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mw9A">[</span>27<span class="cite-bracket" id="mw9Q">]</span></span></a></sup> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_embedding" title="Word embedding" id="mw9g">word embeddings</a> to capture semantic properties of words.</p>

<p id="mw9w">Intermediate tasks (e.g., part-of-speech tagging and dependency parsing) are not needed anymore.</p>

<p id="mw-A"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neural_machine_translation" title="Neural machine translation" id="mw-Q">Neural machine translation</a>, based on the then-newly invented <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seq2seq" title="Seq2seq" id="mw-g">sequence-to-sequence</a> transformations, made obsolete the intermediate steps, such as word alignment, previously necessary for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statistical_machine_translation" title="Statistical machine translation" id="mw-w">statistical machine translation</a>.</p>

</section></section><section data-mw-section-id="7" id="mw_A" aria-labelledby="Common_NLP_tasks"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading2"><h2 id="Common_NLP_tasks">Common NLP tasks</h2><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Common NLP tasks"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<p id="mw_Q">The following is a list of some of the most commonly researched tasks in natural language processing. Some of these tasks have direct real-world applications, while others more commonly serve as subtasks that are used to aid in solving larger tasks.</p>

<p id="mw_g">Though natural language processing tasks are closely intertwined, they can be subdivided into categories for convenience. A coarse division is given below.</p>

<section data-mw-section-id="8" id="mw_w" aria-labelledby="Text_and_speech_processing"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Text_and_speech_processing">Text and speech processing</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Text and speech processing"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAQA"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hebrew_stop_words_word_cloud.png" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAQE"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hebrew_stop_words_word_cloud.png" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Hebrew_stop_words_word_cloud.png/250px-Hebrew_stop_words_word_cloud.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8c/Hebrew_stop_words_word_cloud.png/500px-Hebrew_stop_words_word_cloud.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="852" data-file-height="432" data-file-type="bitmap" height="127" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAQI"></a><figcaption id="mwAQM">Word cloud of stop words in Hebrew</figcaption></figure>
<dl id="mwAQQ"><dt id="mwAQU"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optical_character_recognition" title="Optical character recognition" id="mwAQY">Optical character recognition</a> (OCR)</dt>
<dd id="mwAQc">Given an image representing printed text, determine the corresponding text.</dd></dl>

<dl id="mwAQg"><dt id="mwAQk"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speech_recognition" title="Speech recognition" id="mwAQo">Speech recognition</a></dt><dd id="mwAQs">Given a sound clip of a person or people speaking, determine the textual representation of the speech.  This is the opposite of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Text_to_speech" title="Text to speech" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAQw">text to speech</a> and is one of the extremely difficult problems colloquially termed "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AI-complete" title="AI-complete" id="mwAQ0">AI-complete</a>" (see above).  In <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_speech" title="Natural speech" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAQ4">natural speech</a> there are hardly any pauses between successive words, and thus <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speech_segmentation" title="Speech segmentation" id="mwAQ8">speech segmentation</a> is a necessary subtask of speech recognition (see below). In most spoken languages, the sounds representing successive letters blend into each other in a process termed <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coarticulation" title="Coarticulation" id="mwARA">coarticulation</a>, so the conversion of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analog_signal" title="Analog signal" id="mwARE">analog signal</a> to discrete characters can be a very difficult process. Also, given that words in the same language are spoken by people with different accents, the speech recognition software must be able to recognize the wide variety of input as being identical to each other in terms of its textual equivalent.</dd>
<dt id="mwARI"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speech_segmentation" title="Speech segmentation" id="mwARM">Speech segmentation</a></dt><dd id="mwARQ">Given a sound clip of a person or people speaking, separate it into words.  A subtask of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speech_recognition" title="Speech recognition" id="mwARU">speech recognition</a> and typically grouped with it.</dd></dl>

<dl id="mwARY"><dt id="mwARc"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Text-to-speech" title="Text-to-speech" class="mw-redirect" id="mwARg">Text-to-speech</a></dt>
<dd id="mwARk">Given a text, transform those units and produce a spoken representation. Text-to-speech can be used to aid the visually impaired.<sup about="#mwt93" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-28" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-28&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-28" id="mwARo"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwARs"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwARw">[</span>28<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAR0">]</span></span></a></sup></dd></dl>

<dl id="mwAR4"><dt id="mwAR8"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_segmentation" title="Word segmentation" class="mw-redirect" id="mwASA">Word segmentation</a> (<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokenization_(lexical_analysis)" title="Tokenization (lexical analysis)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwASE">Tokenization</a>)</dt>
<dd id="mwASI"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokenization_(data_security)" title="Tokenization (data security)" id="mwASM">Tokenization</a> is a text-processing technique that divides text into individual words or word fragments. This technique results in two key components: a word index and tokenized text. The word index is a list that maps unique words to specific numerical identifiers, and the tokenized text replaces each word with its corresponding numerical token. These numerical tokens are then used in various deep learning methods.<sup about="#mwt94" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-:0_29-0" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;:0&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-:0-29" id="mwASQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwASU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwASY">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwASc">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>
<dd id="mwASg">For a language like <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language" title="English language" id="mwASk">English</a>, this is fairly trivial, since words are usually separated by spaces. However, some written languages like <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language" title="Chinese language" id="mwASo">Chinese</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_language" title="Japanese language" id="mwASs">Japanese</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thai_language" title="Thai language" id="mwASw">Thai</a> do not mark word boundaries in such a fashion, and in those languages text segmentation is a significant task requiring knowledge of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocabulary" title="Vocabulary" id="mwAS0">vocabulary</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphology_(linguistics)" title="Morphology (linguistics)" id="mwAS4">morphology</a> of words in the language. Sometimes this process is also used in cases like <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bag_of_words" title="Bag of words" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAS8">bag of words</a> (BOW) creation in data mining.<sup about="#mwt97" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-30" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-30&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-30" id="mwATA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwATE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwATI">[</span>30<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwATM">]</span></span></a></sup><sup about="#mwt100" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-31" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-31&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-31" id="mwATQ"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwATU"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwATY">[</span>31<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwATc">]</span></span></a></sup></dd></dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="9" id="mwATg" aria-labelledby="Morphological_analysis"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Morphological_analysis">Morphological analysis</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Morphological analysis"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwATk"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Eustagger_euskal_lematizatzailearen_adibidea.png" class="mw-file-description" id="mwATo"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Eustagger_euskal_lematizatzailearen_adibidea.png" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Eustagger_euskal_lematizatzailearen_adibidea.png/250px-Eustagger_euskal_lematizatzailearen_adibidea.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/27/Eustagger_euskal_lematizatzailearen_adibidea.png/500px-Eustagger_euskal_lematizatzailearen_adibidea.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="1249" data-file-height="474" data-file-type="bitmap" height="95" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwATs"></a><figcaption id="mwATw">Lemmatization of Basque words</figcaption></figure>
<dl id="mwAT0"><dt id="mwAT4"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lemmatisation" title="Lemmatisation" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAT8">Lemmatization</a></dt><dd id="mwAUA">The task of removing inflectional endings only and returning the base dictionary form of a word which is also known as a lemma. Lemmatization is another technique for reducing words to their normalized form. But in this case, the transformation actually uses a dictionary to map words to their actual form.<sup about="#mwt103" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-32" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-32&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-32" id="mwAUE"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAUI"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAUM">[</span>32<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAUQ">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>
<dt id="mwAUU"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphology_(linguistics)" title="Morphology (linguistics)" id="mwAUY">Morphological segmentation</a></dt><dd id="mwAUc">Separate words into individual <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morpheme" title="Morpheme" id="mwAUg">morphemes</a> and identify the class of the morphemes. The difficulty of this task depends greatly on the complexity of the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morphology_(linguistics)" title="Morphology (linguistics)" id="mwAUk">morphology</a> (<i id="mwAUo">i.e.</i>, the structure of words) of the language being considered. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language" title="English language" id="mwAUs">English</a> has fairly simple morphology, especially <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflectional_morphology" title="Inflectional morphology" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAUw">inflectional morphology</a>, and thus it is often possible to ignore this task entirely and simply model all possible forms of a word (e.g., "open, opens, opened, opening") as separate words. In languages such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language" title="Turkish language" id="mwAU0">Turkish</a> or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meitei_language" title="Meitei language" id="mwAU4">Meitei</a>, a highly <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agglutination" title="Agglutination" id="mwAU8">agglutinated</a> Indian language, however, such an approach is not possible, as each dictionary entry has thousands of possible word forms.<sup about="#mwt107" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-33" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-33&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-33" id="mwAVA"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAVE"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAVI">[</span>33<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAVM">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>
<dt id="mwAVQ"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Part-of-speech_tagging" title="Part-of-speech tagging" id="mwAVU">Part-of-speech tagging</a></dt><dd id="mwAVY">Given a sentence, determine the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Part_of_speech" title="Part of speech" id="mwAVc">part of speech</a> (POS) for each word. Many words, especially common ones, can serve as multiple parts of speech. For example, "book" can be a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noun" title="Noun" id="mwAVg">noun</a> ("the book on the table") or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verb" title="Verb" id="mwAVk">verb</a> ("to book a flight"); "set" can be a noun, verb or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjective" title="Adjective" id="mwAVo">adjective</a>; and "out" can be any of at least five different parts of speech.</dd></dl>

<dl id="mwAVs"><dt id="mwAVw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stemming" title="Stemming" id="mwAV0">Stemming</a></dt>
<dd id="mwAV4">The process of reducing inflected (or sometimes derived) words to a base form (e.g., "close" will be the root for "closed", "closing", "close", "closer" etc.). Stemming yields similar results as lemmatization, but does so on grounds of rules, not a dictionary.</dd></dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="10" id="mwAV8" aria-labelledby="Syntactic_analysis"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Syntactic_analysis">Syntactic analysis</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Syntactic analysis"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<span class="mw-empty-elt" about="#mwt108" typeof="mw:Transclusion" id="mwAWA" data-mw="{&quot;parts&quot;:[{&quot;template&quot;:{&quot;target&quot;:{&quot;wt&quot;:&quot;Formal languages&quot;,&quot;href&quot;:&quot;./Template:Formal_languages&quot;},&quot;params&quot;:{},&quot;i&quot;:0}}]}"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" about="#mwt109" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Hlist/styles.css&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1246091330" about="#mwt110" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Module:Sidebar/styles.css&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}"></span><table class="sidebar sidebar-collapse nomobile nowraplinks hlist" role="navigation" about="#mwt108" id="mwAWE"><tbody><tr><td class="sidebar-pretitle">Part of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category:Formal_languages" title="Category:Formal languages"><i>a series</i></a> on</td></tr><tr><th class="sidebar-title-with-pretitle"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_languages" title="Formal languages" class="mw-redirect">Formal languages</a></th></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content" style="padding-top:0.2em;">
<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:#ddddff;text-align:center;;color: var(--color-base)"><div class="sidebar-list-title-c">Key concepts</div></div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content">
<ul><li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_system" title="Formal system">Formal system</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphabet_(formal_languages)" title="Alphabet (formal languages)">Alphabet</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syntax_(logic)" title="Syntax (logic)">Syntax</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semantics_of_logic" title="Semantics of logic" class="mw-redirect">Formal semantics</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semantics_(computer_science)" title="Semantics (computer science)" class="mw-redirect">Semantics (programming languages)</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_grammar" title="Formal grammar">Formal grammar</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formation_rule" title="Formation rule">Formation rule</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Well-formed_formula" title="Well-formed formula">Well-formed formula</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automata_theory" title="Automata theory">Automata theory</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regular_expression" title="Regular expression">Regular expression</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Production_(computer_science)" title="Production (computer science)">Production</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_expression" title="Ground expression">Ground expression</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomic_formula" title="Atomic formula">Atomic formula</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-content" style="padding-top:0.2em;">
<div class="sidebar-list mw-collapsible mw-collapsed"><div class="sidebar-list-title" style="background:#ddddff;text-align:center;;color: var(--color-base)"><div class="sidebar-list-title-c">Applications</div></div><div class="sidebar-list-content mw-collapsible-content">
<ul><li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_methods" title="Formal methods">Formal methods</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propositional_calculus" title="Propositional calculus" class="mw-redirect">Propositional calculus</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predicate_logic" title="Predicate logic" class="mw-redirect">Predicate logic</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_notation" title="Mathematical notation">Mathematical notation</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_language_processing" class="mw-selflink selflink">Natural language processing</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programming_language_theory" title="Programming language theory">Programming language theory</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_linguistics" title="Mathematical linguistics">Mathematical linguistics</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computational_linguistics" title="Computational linguistics">Computational linguistics</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syntax_analysis" title="Syntax analysis" class="mw-redirect">Syntax analysis</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_verification" title="Formal verification">Formal verification</a></li>
<li><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Automated_theorem_proving" title="Automated theorem proving">Automated theorem proving</a></li></ul></div></div></td>
</tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><span class="mw-empty-elt"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1333133064" about="#mwt111" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Hlist/styles.css&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1239400231" about="#mwt112" typeof="mw:Extension/templatestyles" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;templatestyles&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;src&quot;:&quot;Module:Navbar/styles.css&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;extsrc&quot;:&quot;&quot;}}"></span><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template:Formal_languages" title="Template:Formal languages"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk:Formal_languages?action=edit&amp;redlink=1" title="Template talk:Formal languages (page does not exist)" class="new" typeof="mw:LocalizedAttrs" data-mw-i18n="{&quot;title&quot;:{&quot;lang&quot;:&quot;x-page&quot;,&quot;key&quot;:&quot;red-link-title&quot;,&quot;params&quot;:[&quot;Template talk:Formal languages&quot;]}}"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Template:Formal_languages" title="Special:EditPage/Template:Formal languages"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<dl id="mwAWI"><dt id="mwAWM"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammar_induction" title="Grammar induction" id="mwAWQ">Grammar induction</a><sup about="#mwt115" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-34" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-34&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-34" id="mwAWU"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAWY"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAWc">[</span>34<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAWg">]</span></span></a></sup></dt>
<dd id="mwAWk">Generate a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_grammar" title="Formal grammar" id="mwAWo">formal grammar</a> that describes a language's syntax.</dd>
<dt id="mwAWs"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentence_breaking" title="Sentence breaking" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAWw">Sentence breaking</a> (also known as "<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentence_boundary_disambiguation" title="Sentence boundary disambiguation" id="mwAW0">sentence boundary disambiguation</a>")</dt>
<dd id="mwAW4">Given a chunk of text, find the sentence boundaries. Sentence boundaries are often marked by <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_stop" title="Full stop" id="mwAW8">periods</a> or other <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punctuation_mark" title="Punctuation mark" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAXA">punctuation marks</a>, but these same characters can serve other purposes (e.g., marking <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbreviation" title="Abbreviation" id="mwAXE">abbreviations</a>).</dd>
<dt id="mwAXI"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsing" title="Parsing" id="mwAXM">Parsing</a></dt><dd id="mwAXQ">Determine the <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parse_tree" title="Parse tree" id="mwAXU">parse tree</a> (grammatical analysis) of a given sentence. The <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grammar" title="Grammar" id="mwAXY">grammar</a> for <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_language" title="Natural language" id="mwAXc">natural languages</a> is <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambiguous" title="Ambiguous" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAXg">ambiguous</a> and typical sentences have multiple possible analyses: perhaps surprisingly, for a typical sentence there may be thousands of potential parses (most of which will seem completely nonsensical to a human). There are two primary types of parsing: <i id="mwAXk">dependency parsing</i> and <i id="mwAXo">constituency parsing</i>. Dependency parsing focuses on the relationships between words in a sentence (marking things like primary objects and predicates), whereas constituency parsing focuses on building out the parse tree using a <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Probabilistic_context-free_grammar" title="Probabilistic context-free grammar" id="mwAXs">probabilistic context-free grammar</a> (PCFG) (see also <i id="mwAXw"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stochastic_grammar" title="Stochastic grammar" id="mwAX0">stochastic grammar</a></i>).</dd></dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="11" id="mwAX4" aria-labelledby="Lexical_semantics_(of_individual_words_in_context)"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Lexical_semantics_(of_individual_words_in_context)"><span id="Lexical_semantics_.28of_individual_words_in_context.29" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Lexical semantics (of individual words in context)</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Lexical semantics (of individual words in context)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb" id="mwAX8"><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Entity_Linking_-_Example_of_pipeline.png" class="mw-file-description" id="mwAYA"><img resource="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Entity_Linking_-_Example_of_pipeline.png" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Entity_Linking_-_Example_of_pipeline.png/250px-Entity_Linking_-_Example_of_pipeline.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail" decoding="async" loading="lazy" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/34/Entity_Linking_-_Example_of_pipeline.png/500px-Entity_Linking_-_Example_of_pipeline.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&amp;utm_campaign=parser&amp;utm_content=thumbnail 2x" data-file-width="12051" data-file-height="2943" data-file-type="bitmap" height="61" width="250" class="mw-file-element" id="mwAYE"></a><figcaption id="mwAYI">An entity linking pipeline</figcaption></figure>
<dl id="mwAYM"><dt id="mwAYQ"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexical_semantics" title="Lexical semantics" id="mwAYU">Lexical semantics</a></dt><dd id="mwAYY">What is the computational meaning of individual words in context?</dd>
<dt id="mwAYc"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Distributional_semantics" title="Distributional semantics" id="mwAYg">Distributional semantics</a></dt><dd id="mwAYk">How can we learn semantic representations from data?</dd>
<dt id="mwAYo"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Named_entity_recognition" title="Named entity recognition" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAYs">Named entity recognition</a> (NER)</dt><dd id="mwAYw">Given a stream of text, determine which items in the text map to proper names, such as people or places, and what the type of each such name is (e.g. person, location, organization). Although <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalization" title="Capitalization" id="mwAY0">capitalization</a> can aid in recognizing named entities in languages such as English, this information cannot aid in determining the type of <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Named_entity" title="Named entity" id="mwAY4">named entity</a>, and in any case, is often inaccurate or insufficient.  For example, the first letter of a sentence is also capitalized, and named entities often span several words, only some of which are capitalized.  Furthermore, many other languages in non-Western scripts (e.g. <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language" title="Chinese language" id="mwAY8">Chinese</a> or <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language" title="Arabic language" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAZA">Arabic</a>) do not have any capitalization at all, and even languages with capitalization may not consistently use it to distinguish names. For example, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language" title="German language" id="mwAZE">German</a> capitalizes all <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noun" title="Noun" id="mwAZI">nouns</a>, regardless of whether they are names, and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language" title="French language" id="mwAZM">French</a> and <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language" id="mwAZQ">Spanish</a> do not capitalize names that serve as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjective" title="Adjective" id="mwAZU">adjectives</a>. This task is also referred to as token classification.<sup about="#mwt118" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-35" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-35&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-35" id="mwAZY"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAZc"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAZg">[</span>35<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAZk">]</span></span></a></sup></dd></dl>

<dl id="mwAZo"><dt id="mwAZs"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentiment_analysis" title="Sentiment analysis" id="mwAZw">Sentiment analysis</a> (see also <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multimodal_sentiment_analysis" title="Multimodal sentiment analysis" id="mwAZ0">Multimodal sentiment analysis</a>)</dt>
<dd id="mwAZ4">Sentiment analysis involves identifying and classifying the emotional tone expressed in text. This technique involves analyzing text to determine whether the expressed sentiment is positive, negative, or neutral. Models for sentiment classification typically utilize inputs such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word_n-gram_language_model" title="Word n-gram language model" id="mwAZ8">word n-grams</a>, <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Term_frequency-inverse_document_frequency" title="Term frequency-inverse document frequency" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAaA">Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency</a> (TF-IDF) features, hand-generated features, or employ <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_learning" title="Deep learning" id="mwAaE">deep learning</a> models designed to recognize both long-term and short-term dependencies in text sequences. The applications of sentiment analysis are diverse, extending to tasks such as categorizing customer reviews on various online platforms.<sup about="#mwt121" class="mw-ref reference" id="cite_ref-:0_29-1" rel="dc:references" typeof="mw:Extension/ref" data-mw="{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;ref&quot;,&quot;attrs&quot;:{&quot;name&quot;:&quot;:0&quot;},&quot;body&quot;:{&quot;id&quot;:&quot;mw-reference-text-cite_note-:0-29&quot;}}"><a href="#cite_note-:0-29" id="mwAaI"><span class="mw-reflink-text" id="mwAaM"><span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAaQ">[</span>29<span class="cite-bracket" id="mwAaU">]</span></span></a></sup></dd>
<dt id="mwAaY"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terminology_extraction" title="Terminology extraction" id="mwAac">Terminology extraction</a></dt>
<dd id="mwAag">The goal of terminology extraction is to automatically extract relevant terms from a given corpus.</dd>
<dt id="mwAak"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Word-sense_disambiguation" title="Word-sense disambiguation" id="mwAao">Word-sense disambiguation</a> (WSD)</dt><dd id="mwAas">Many words have more than one <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meaning_(linguistics)" title="Meaning (linguistics)" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAaw">meaning</a>; we have to select the meaning which makes the most sense in context.  For this problem, we are typically given a list of words and associated word senses, e.g. from a dictionary or an online resource such as <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WordNet" title="WordNet" id="mwAa0">WordNet</a>.</dd>
<dt id="mwAa4"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entity_linking" title="Entity linking" id="mwAa8">Entity linking</a></dt><dd id="mwAbA">Many words—typically proper names—refer to <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Named_entity" title="Named entity" id="mwAbE">named entities</a>; here we have to select the entity (a famous individual, a location, a company, etc.) which is referred to in context.</dd></dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="12" id="mwAbI" aria-labelledby="Relational_semantics_(semantics_of_individual_sentences)"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Relational_semantics_(semantics_of_individual_sentences)"><span id="Relational_semantics_.28semantics_of_individual_sentences.29" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Relational semantics (semantics of individual sentences)</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Relational semantics (semantics of individual sentences)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
<dl id="mwAbM"><dt id="mwAbQ"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relationship_extraction" title="Relationship extraction" id="mwAbU">Relationship extraction</a></dt><dd id="mwAbY">Given a chunk of text, identify the relationships among named entities (e.g. who is married to whom).</dd>
<dt id="mwAbc"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semantic_parsing" title="Semantic parsing" id="mwAbg">Semantic parsing</a></dt><dd id="mwAbk">Given a piece of text (typically a sentence), produce a formal representation of its semantics, either as a graph (e.g., in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abstract_Meaning_Representation" title="Abstract Meaning Representation" id="mwAbo">AMR parsing</a>) or in accordance with a logical formalism (e.g., in <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discourse_representation_theory" title="Discourse representation theory" id="mwAbs">DRT parsing</a>). This challenge typically includes aspects of several more elementary NLP tasks from semantics (e.g., semantic role labelling, word-sense disambiguation) and can be extended to include full-fledged discourse analysis (e.g., discourse analysis, coreference; see <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="#Natural_language_understanding" class="mw-selflink-fragment" id="mwAbw">Natural language understanding</a> below).</dd>
<dt id="mwAb0"><a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semantic_role_labeling" title="Semantic role labeling" id="mwAb4">Semantic role labelling</a> (see also implicit semantic role labelling below)</dt>
<dd id="mwAb8">Given a single sentence, identify and disambiguate semantic predicates (e.g., verbal <a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frame_semantics_(linguistics)" title="Frame semantics (linguistics)" id="mwAcA">frames</a>), then identify and classify the frame elements (<a rel="mw:WikiLink" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semantic_roles" title="Semantic roles" class="mw-redirect" id="mwAcE">semantic roles</a>).</dd></dl>

</section><section data-mw-section-id="13" id="mwAcI" aria-labelledby="Discourse_(semantics_beyond_individual_sentences)"><div class="mw-heading mw-heading3"><h3 id="Discourse_(semantics_beyond_individual_sentences)"><span id="Discourse_.28semantics_beyond_individual_sentences.29" typeof="mw:FallbackId"></span>Discourse (semantics beyond individual sentences)</h3><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Natural_language_processing&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Discourse (semantics beyond individual sentences)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></div>
